It isnot surprising, says another writer, to 
find the average woman's head of to-day deve- 
loping in size and strength, hence it is not 80 
much of a surprise to us to meet the woman 
whose head will measure, twenty-two inches, 
the normal size of a man's head. In the mod- 
ern maf,as well'as the model woman, the pro- 
port’on from. the opening of the ear to tho 
frontal lobe is lengthening Formerly the 
woman's head, from the opening of the ear to 
the occipital lobe, was longer. 

This measurement does not necessarily 
imply that the characteristics of home making, 
mother love and all that goto make a woman 
womanly are shrinking, as some assert, but 
merely not being developed in proportion to 
those characteristics usually given to man they. 
are ata standstill, 

The coming man and the coming woman aré 
‘developiag in common,. The crown of tha 
head, self-culture and independence the fotes 
head, casuality, which were typical of man’s 
development, are now strongly deyeloped in a 
womans bea. This development has eoabhled 
women to take charge of their own business 
| affairs, toorganize and to lead. This quality of 

organization is inctensing, and much is also 
being done ta developwomen physically, 

Gymnastics ure an imporiant feature of 
woman's education to-day } a ad climbs 
ing, rowlag, golfing, tenaie and al abletic 
sports, Women have greater power of ondu- 
rance than men—for example, the woman who

It has frequently been necessary of-late to 


benefit of those’ high powers whose orbits: 


and who are, no doubt, guided by considera- 
tions- unknown to those mainly affected by 


leave, them voiceless where subjects of vital 
.importance,to them are concerned. Indeed, 
it is their sense of impotence thatoccasional- 
ly touches them: on the raw; but, of course, 
those on higher circles seldom hear, the 
‘mutterings of the ,vulgar herd, and have 
few opportunities of consulting’ those who 
might be in the slightest degree termed 
representative of the working class. The 
latest grievance is found in a Bill which 
appeais’ in the Agenda of the, Legisla- 
as follows:— 
“First reading ‘of a Bill entitled an Or- 
dinance to transfer to the General Revenne 
the moneys standing in the ‘Treasury to 
the credit of the Widows’ and Orphans’ 
Pension Fund, to provide for future payment 
of pensions to the Widows and Orphans of 
deceased Public Officers, and to ‘make the 
payment of the same a charge upon and 
payable out of the said General: Revenue.” 
That Bill has been on the agenda forthe 
last two meetings, but it has been post. 
poned on cach occasion, and, ‘curiously 
cnough, no copy of the proposed Or- 
That 
in itself. is a peculiar circumstance, which 
we need not dilate upon at’ this stage. 
Nut coming to+thd character of the Bill 
Jas set forth in the title, we strike an old- 
standing. grievance—the change from a bad 
system to a worse, ‘This Widows’ and 
Orphans’ Pension Fund has almost in- 
variably been the subject of the greatest 
Ina few 
cases it has proved, we cannot doubt, bene: 
ficial; but.in the’ majority it has proved 
the civil 
officers of the Colony could be lawfully 
bled by. the Government—in. other words, 
it has proved an official " squeeze.” We 
will submit sume illustrations of the work- 
support of our 
contention. ‘Tq begin with it must be re- 
membered that there is nothing voluntary 
about the Fund; itis a compulsory order 
that civil servants must pay a certain per. 
centage, of their monthly salarics to the 
Fund, no matter whether they expect to 
Take the cas¢ of a 
married man who has been in the service 
for a period of 15 of 20 years; his wife and 
children predecease him, and in disgust at 
the malificent effects of the Colony he 
decides to retire ‘from. the service and to 
return to bis native land, All these years 
he has been contributivg month after month 
to the Widows’ and Orphans’ Pension Fund, 
but now when he has resolved to retire all 
In 
other words, lie is not entitled to a penny of 
the money which, it may be, against his will, 
certainly without his. voluntary consent, he 
has been contributing to the coffers of the 
He has been mulcted of that 
money, and has compulsorily made a gift of 
some 4 per cent. of his monthly salary to 
Had that civil servant 
paid the money into an insurance so- 
ciety, the money would have been re- 
tumed to tim on the maturing of the 
wife 
and family; or at hisdeath it would have 
gone to swell his estate. But the money 
would not have beer ifretrievably lost, a3 
in the case of the Government Fund, 
There would, besides, have been the accru- 
ed interest to gather, but We may waive that 


tributions during that period are absolutely 
lost. If he be a single man the Government 
waxes generous and-sefunds him half, the 
total amount of money he lias contributed, 
so that whatever happens the Government is 
onthe safe side. The hardship to the civil 
_|servant is only a question of , degree ; the 
“squeeze” has been madé. There is another 
In 
the particular case which we have in mind, 
the widow is entitled to the magnificent sum 
of Something like $5 per annum paid month- 
ly from the’ Widows' and Orphans’ Fund. 
That is to say, she. has to call once a month 
atthe Treaury for het 42 cents, Before 
reat % ‘ % 


the poof wonian has coma ‘to town, waited 
at the Treasury and taken’ a chair. or-a car 
home ~ there. ‘can , be: very’: little left 
of that 42° cents, The thing is ridiculous ; 
but it illustrates the methods of the Govern- 
‘went, which is neither just nor generous,

as the Colony increases in population and: 
"the Civil Service—as. a natural consequence 
—expands, ‘There are rio statistics before us

of ‘contributors. to the, Fund; but-we may 
take it that the present average will be maih- 
-| tained, In that case; and taking into account’ 
the rate of increase in the. Colony's popula 
tion, it will not be surprising if within the 
‘| next half century the present accumulated 
sum would have grown to ,#1,000,00—- 
representing the contributions deducted 
from the salarics of Government officials. 
Now, we feel confident .that many » of 
those civil officers would far rather invest 
their money ih annuities in any :of the 
numerous insurance societies which are so 
teady nowadays toroffer 4 maximum of re- 
turn for a minimum of outlay.’ Byits policy 
up to the present time, the Government has 
made a clear gain of some lakhs, of dollats, 
and the process is to continue on even more 
advantageous (erms-~-advantageous for the 
Government we may be sure. Were the 
accumulated funds, which properly belong 
‘to the civil servants, invested in the Colony 
we are: quite certain that safe mortgages 
bearing interest at the rate of 7 per cent. per 
annum could be secured, furnishing a return 
not short of some $15,000 a year’on'the 
existing Fund..° Phat’sum we may take “it 
would be sufficiently adejuate to provide. for 
those entided to draw on the Fund. As itis, 
Jooking at the proposition soberly, we cannot 
see where these contributions aretoend, As* 
the years roll on, the Fund would increase 
enormously until ‘contributions would no 
longer be necessary, the interest on the 
accumulated sum being more than enough 
to mect all requirements. - If in such b case 
the demand for contributions were suspend- 
ed, 2 great injustice would be done to those 
“whose contributions in the past had been 
confiscated. So to get over that difficulty, 
to hide their traces, the Government calmly 
throw the whole amount ‘into General’ 
Revenue and neither the civil servant nor 
anybody else can tell then what has happen- 
ed tothe money. A really great scheme, 
indeed ; the originator is a born actwary, and 
one whose servicesare of supreme importance 
We cannot go ‘into 
further details an this question on account 
of the limited space at our disposal. The 
subject is, as we haye shown, hedged about 
with intricacies, and will involve a good’ 
The scheme, as we 
have pointed out, is wholly bad; it is 
the Government 
alone; all others are dealt with as’ so 
many pawns who are only tolerated be- 
Tt is hardly’ pos- 
sible for any one to say a good word far the 
scheme, and we do nol suppose any: of the 
elected members of the Legislative Council

Point and knocked down a market gardener, 
who sustained a fenclured leg, as already 
recorded in thete columns, was resumed 
before Mr. G. N.Orme this morning, when

i . Mr, Alex, Hotie isa traveller as well’ as his. reson 
Majesty’s Corsul-Genoral:, at Chengtu, ‘the, 
capital“of China's largest province." In 1883 
he journeyed. along the Thibetan road from 
Chengtu and crossed into Yunoan tittle dream: 
ing (hat twenty years later he would again find 
himself in Stechuan facing westwards, But 
such proved to be the case, and a’ diary ,of his 
travels over close upon: fourteen hundced miles 
appéars in the form of'a White Paper issued to 
Parliament. ‘The traveller wriles of his experi- 
ences with a lively sense’ of their romantic 
nature and with distinct literary ‘skill, His 
interesting ‘volume, which consists of 80 
closely-printed pages, is issued as. “China 
No, & (1995)." Report by Mr, A. Hosie, his’ 
Majesty's Contul-General at Chengtu, on o 
joumey to the Eastern Frontier of Thibet (with 
La map).’* Setting out from Chengtu, where 
one can observea confused mass of greenery 
lit up in February and March by the bright 
yellow blossoms of the rape, and in. May aod. 
August by golden, patches af ripening grain, 
Mr.:Hosie journeyed on the great highway 
which connects the city with Lhasa, and for 
the first diys passed with his caravan through 
acountry riddled with irrigating streams and 
canals, with alders fringing -every watercourse 
and beans blossoming in the dikes between 
the ‘paddy ‘plots. By this same road travel 
the Chinese Imperial. Residents for Thibet ; | 
along it devout pilgrims, eager to look upon 
, THE FACE OF THE DALAL LAMA, 

- Some of the latter take years’ over the. jour- 
ney, but the Imperial couriers, with’ relays of 
horses, are able to carry messages from Lhasa 
—to the ‘nearest telegraph office, 1,300 miles 
away, at Tachien-lu—in les than 20 days. 
The road is barred by lofty mountain ranges, 
which strike terror into the superstitious way- 
farers, and so narrow are some of the spurs that 
one looks down,into an abyss on either side of 
the roadway, while in places the path zigzags 
lackwards and forwards over a face of small, 
loose, slippery slories und at times descends 
into gorges where Nature has ran riot, Mr. 
Hosic describes everything that he sees, and 
throws new light on the country and its people.” 
Un the road to Tachien-lu he visited O-mei 
Haien and went to the top—a height of 10,358 
feet, The sights. to’ be ‘seen from the summit 
are the “Glory of Buddha'—the reflection of 
the sun on the clouds hanging on the abyss 
below the top of the precipice; the Myriad 
Lights,” or lamps’ which are visible at night in 
the gulf underneath; andthe Snowy Mountains

' A LONDON’ wire of the Sth inst, says.—The 
Times in a lending article comments upon the 
interesting administration reporton Kelanten, 
It pays a warm tribute of praise lo the reforms 
effected there, under the beneficent administra- 
tion and control of the British advisers to the 
Rajah of that State. In-its opinion there is no 
portion {the world where the inherent aptitude 
of Britons for governing Oricntals has been 
more conspicuously displayed than in Malaysia 
where consequenlly there is unchecked and ins. 
‘creasing prosperity, The natural advantages 
of Kelantan, so the Y¥mes points out, ‘are such 
that if it be only moderately well-governed, the 
state will become, at no distant time, one of 
‘the most prosperous countries in the Malay 
Peninsula. ae 


“Te Yellow Dragon for August, which js now 
‘to hind, is the fist number of volume seven of 
this interesting Queen's College publication, 
The head master’s address an “break-up day” 
is published in full and as.it contains many 
instructive suggestiods, should be read and ree 
rend by the scholars. One of the old pupils, 
Hung Hing Kam, who is now studying in Lon 
don, has written a lengthy letter to a mas(er,.in 
the course of which he expresses the opinion 
that, after what he has seen of English Public 
Schools, Queen's College, with its ever-increas- 
ing number of boys, is hopelessly behind, so far 
as sport is conterned. He dots not wish to 
advocate sport tothe detriment.of the school 
work of the boys, but considers that, as at pre- 
sent, the lack of physical exercite amongst the 
greater bulk of the Chinese boys is most de- 
plorable,

AT THE ROUND (RY LINE, & 

Pressing over many pages of this interesting 
nanative of a journey through a wonderful 
country we arrive ot the stage where 
Mr. Hosié’s caravan, reached the steep 
hillside whose ridge marks the boundary 
between China’ and Thibet. Mr. Hosie 
describes how he met the Inma himself travel- 
ling in a green sedan chair borne by four Chi- 
nese bearers, with retiel bearers and escorted 
by a retinue of from thirty to forty men, Thi- 
belan and Chinese, some of the latter with but: | 
tons on their official hats. ‘There was a run- 
ning fire of questions as to their respective de»- 
tinations, and one grey-bearded old man asked 
Mr, Hosie whether he. was going into Thibet. 
When he answered that he was proceeding 
only as far.asthe frontier, he said, You have 
not far to go,” and such proved to be the case, 
for he soon arrived at the top of a steep hill- 
side the ridge of which forms the boundary 
line between the two countries. Under this 
brow he was met~by the Chinese authorit’es, 
who again begged him not to cross the 
boundary line, which lay a few yards above him. 

, AN INVASION RESISTED, 

‘Having beea reassured thot he had not-the 
slightest intention of setting afoot in.Thibet, 
they begged him to waita few minutes, until 
they made the necessary disposition of troops 
along the boundary line, which was guarded 
on the Thibetan side by armed sofdiers, Ina 
few minutes he was invited to ascend the brow, 
of the hill, where be: found: the Thibetan 
soldiers arrarged in a line running: north-east 
and south-west, In front of the Thibetan 
soldiers another line of Szechuan Thibetan 
soldiers, equal in number to the laiter, was 
drawn up. The Thibetan soldiers, who were 
armed with matchlocks and swords, were un- 
der the command of a Chief, and a lama with 
a. gilt pagoda hat stood by his side, There 
was perfect silence as, escorted by the Chinese 
authorities, he went up to the boundary stone 
which marks the frontier of the two countries, 
but’ he could see a fixed determination on the 
faces of tho troops to resist any attempt on his 
part to cross into Thibet. The boundary 
stone, which stands some. 30 yards to ‘the 
north-east of an obo by the roadside, is'a well. 
worn, four-sided pillar of sandstone, about 3 
feet in height, cach side measuring some 38 
inches. There was no ingcription on thestone,: 
and when unthinkingly he made a movement 
to look for. writing on the Thibetan side, the 
‘Chinese officials at once stepped in front of |” 
him and barred the road to Thibet.y * ae 


YrunG CHAU KWONG surrendered to his bail 
this morning in the case in which he is charged 
with embezzlement of various sums of money 
received by him on behalf of Ip Tin Ting, 
‘details: of ‘which have already appeared to 
“these columns, Mr. C. F. Dixon, of Mr,John 
Hastings’ office, appeared for the prosecution; 
andMr. H.N. Ferrers, instructed by Mr. Otto 
Kong Sing, forthe defence, Mr. Ferrers said 
he had no evidence to call, andasked that his 
Warship. discharge the accused as all the 
ingredients constituting the crime of embexzle- 
ment were lacking, The accused was nota 
salaried Servant of the complainant ; he was an 
independent broker, employed by him on the 
usual commission, He did not conceal the 
receipt of the money; he did not run away; 
and he was thereall along making up the ac- 
counts,’ getting ‘orders for, and collecting bills 
of, the complainants. He took the money on 
account and ‘told complainant so; therefore 
there was an entle absence of criminal intent, 
and-no case against his client—Mr.° Dixon 
submitted that all the ingredients of an em- 
bezlement were present. Accused went and 
collected money on behalf of complainant, and 
‘] never made any report, nor did he account for 
the sums so received.—His Worship said he 
would consider the point as to whether defend- 
antwas a salaried servant or not, and reserved 
his decision unlil Monday afternoon. ‘De- 
fendant was allowed bail as belore, $750.

vee & _Lonnon, s4th ‘September. 

_ The feeling between Norway-and, Sweden. 
is apparently again most strained. ._ 

‘An authoritative announcement. in Stock- 
holm accuses Norway of. wilfully: deceiving 
the foreign press on the subject of the de- 
‘molition of the frontier f.rtifications, while 
the Norwegians declare that Sweden is now 
aking the conditions harder thai had been 
previously decided.on, > ° 
‘{Late’ advices from Christiania on the sub- 

. Ject of the diss alution show that the Norwegian’ 
+.) people; ina referendum vote taken on 13th 
. ult, pronounced in favour of a separation. with 
” Sweden with remarkable though not unexpoct- 
+ ed voanimity. Of 450,000 voters, 320,000 
cast ballots. While the full result’ was not 

": known lor some hours, ip to midnight returns 
showed that about one person in three thousand 
Voted against the diseolution. -The differerice 
belween the total nuniber of votersand the 
number of votes cast was attributable to ab- 

» — Sentees, such as sailors abroad and others’ who 
i were outof the country at the time. There were 
 geenes of the greatest enthusinsm- everywhere. 

" Thousands of .women.who did not have the 
sight of franchise signe) petitions in the streets 
indorsing the dissolution. In Christiania and. 
other cities the total population turned out, 
everyone wearing long streamers of the nation- 
mal colours, and pictures of Premier Michelsen. 
“One of the niemb:rs of the Gabinent said, 
that evening" Che result surpasses tho most 

, sanguine expectations, . The next official steps 
_ will betaken aftertheSihorthing meetson August 
gist, when the resultof the plebiscite will be 
communicated to Sweden through the Siedish 
Cabinet and the Riksdag. The Storthing will. 
repeat the request thatthe Riksdag declare the 
Riksakt in operation and the union dissolved,, 

The Storthing will also express a willingness 

to negotiate concerning the details of the dis- 

- golution, It is the earnest request of Norway 
. to conclude the disspiuion amicably, Norway. 

-will never retract, but everything will bé‘done 

to meet the wishes of Sweden in other: direc 

tions, “If peace depends upon abolishing the 

forts they will ‘be abolished, Norway must 

, remain a monarchy; the peapte do not desire 
a republic” At midnight repoits from 176 
places showed a vole of 95,035 for and thirty. 

seven against dissolution.—Ed, 77.8.7,

ANCIENT RUIN IN. NEW », 
MEXICO.:: } 

Santa Fe (N. M ), August 9.—The ruin of a 
communal building, containing 1500° rooms 
and never before visited by whiie men or.map- 
ped, has been discovered thirty ‘miles’ west of 
Santa Fe and a half mile south of the famous. 

Stone ‘Lions of Cocchita, «© 7 
The discovery was made by A. . Chaycraft 
_of Santa Fe, who was out with Edgar L. Hewitt 
of the ethnological bureau taking photographs. |” ata ajyrd Aug., Gen—Aagaard, 
of the thousands of cliff and cave dwellings to eanenlG Ge na 171g, T, Peteisen, 13th

, The grave of General Sir Hector Macdonald 
in the Dean Cemetery, Edinburgh, has hitherto 
been unmarked, A monument, however, has 
now been'erected by public, subscriptions. tt | 
takes the form of an obelisk, supported by can- 
‘non balls at each corner. In front of the base 
stands apedestal with a bronze bust, which 
shows Sir thector in the uniform of an aid-de- 


one tothe King, with a greatcoat thrown open & Co. 
dug laying his many orders and medals, British Nanshan, Br. s.s , 1,299, A. W. Brynull, rath,

MILLIONAIRE'S WILL. 
et 

Mrs. William Zeigler has commenced an 
action in New York with the object of upsetting 
the will of her, husband, who accumulated a 
vast fortune as the Inventor of baking powder, 
and spent large sums recently in Arctic explo- 
‘gation, The widow is not contented at receiving 
only £10,000 per annum and two. palatial re- 
sidences,‘and seeks a share of the £6,000,000 
feft by the baking powder king to a fifteen. 
years-old boy, whom he adopted as his son. 
She says that her husband was insane at the 
, time. of making his will, or he would have left 
ber a widow's share of his millions instead of a 

‘mere pittance of £10,000 per annum. ©

It is difficult to realise that in time of 
peace and under conditions which were in 
the highest’ degree conducive to safely, the. 
fingship of Admiral ‘Togo should have been 
Idgt, and, with it, g99 lives. The Afitase 

“|... ‘whs a modern warship in every respect ; she 
‘had ‘weathered the gales of the bleak coast 
off the Lisoting Peninsula; had been in 
cvery naval fight and skirmish since the’ be- 
gitining of the war ; and had proved a valu- 
able weapon in the hands of Admiral Togo. 
Yet, when all is over, an accident occurs 
arid a historical career ends disastrously. We 
have no means at present of learning how 
the accident which terminated so tragically 
happened on the Miduse, ‘The. appalling 
“déath-rate shows that there must’ have been 
some strange agency at work, for even in 

. time of war the sinking of a warship is not 
waMgcessarily attended by such startling results, 
When. the 2etropartorek with Admiral 
Makaroff on board ran upon three mines 
“asd sank almost instantaneously, a large 
proportion of the crev was rescued. In 
the case ofthe WMéihaat, however, out of 2. 

. total’ complement of 735, no fewer than 
cer syy arc losl, while the vessel is presumably 
+ fying. at anchor ‘in a busy harbour, ‘The 
_ details of the disaster are so tantalisingly 
meagre that we are compelled to search for 
some explanation other than that given that 

fire broke out on board and caught the 
magazine. A warship of the type of the 

_ Mikasa is specially constructed to withstand 
fire. In battle, the -vetsel is stripped of 
very piece of material that would suggest

return to bis native dand. All these years 
he has been contributiig month after month 
ty the Widows’ and Orphans’ Pension Fund, 
but now when he has’ resolved to retire all 
that money reverts to tie Government: In 
other words, he is not entitled toa penny of 
“the money which, it may be, against his: will, 
certainly without his voluntary consent, be 
has been contributing to the coffers of the 
Government, He has-been mulcted of that 
monty, and has compulsorily made a gilt of 
some 4 per cent. of his, monthly salary to 
the Government. Had thdt civil servant 
paid the money, into an insurance $o- 
ciely, the money would have been re. 
turned to hin ou the maturing of the 
policy, provided he survived his wife 
and family; or at his death it would have 
gone ty swell his estate, © But the money 
would not have been irretrievably lost, as 
in the case of the Government. Fund. 
There would, besides, have been the aceru 
ed interest to gather, but we may waive that 
point al present. ‘Take another illustration, 
where a man resigns from the Service after a 
few years. Tf be be a married man bis con- 
tributions during thal-period are absolutely 
lost... Ibe be a single man the Goverment 
waxes generous and refunds him half the 
total amount of money be has contributed, 
ao that whatever happens the Government is 
unthe safe side, ‘The hardship to the civil 
servantis only a question of degree ; the 
“squeeze” has been made. ‘There is another 
case, in which a widow is concerned. In 
the particular case which we have in mind, 
the widow is entitled to the magnificent sum 
of something like $5 per annum paid month- 
ly from the Widows' and Orphans’ Fund, 
That is to say, she has to call once a month 
at the Treasury for her 42 cents, Before 
the poor wontan has come to town, waited 
at the Treasury aud taken a chair or a car 
home there, canbe very little — left 
of that 42 centsg/sThe thing..is ridiculous ; 
bout it ‘idoarates Eke methods a the Govern: 
ment, which is nedher “just nor generous,

asthe Colony increases in population and

—éxpands. Thereare nostatistics before us

tion, it will not -be surprising if within the

HY MIKARO'S AUTHORITY. 


| JUNK, LIGHTS. 
———— 


1f the Merchant Shipping Amendment 
BIN, which passed through committee of the | 
Legislative Council yesterday, adequately 
deals with the question of junk lights it will 
~ gertainly prove a boon to the shipping in the , 
‘Yiarbour. Time and again, when questions ; 
6f collisions have been before the Courts, in 
Swhich junks were concerned, the chief point 
‘hus turned on the lights carried by the 
native craft, The Judges of the Supreme 
‘Court have repeatedly animadyerted on the 
character of the lights displayed, but without 
having legal provisious permitting the junk: 
inspectors to deal with offenders in this 
‘respect, the remonstrances have gone practi- 
cally ublieeded. Recently a case came up 
before the Puisne Judge, the owner of a 
stdam launch being sued for damages as the 
_ result'of'a collision with a junk, On that 
occasion there was prudiced in Court the 
lamp which it was alleged had been burn- 
ing borllliatifly atthe masthead of the junk.’ 
“A more dilapidated specimen of a lamp 
jt would have been impossible to imagine. 
The ‘wick was a picce of twine, and two sides 
of thic lamp were gone. Yet the occupants 
of the junk strongly asseverated their con-, 
victin that the lamp gave a brilliant light, 
‘and even’ suggested that it was as good a 
‘Tamp,ag the searchtight in, front of steamers 
passing through the Suez Canal. Anybody 
who, saw this paragon among lanips would 
have laughed at the idea that such a lamp 
could have been visibleat two cables’ lengths, 
‘but.not so the junk-owner, When this de- 
generate example of a light was exhibited to 
“the-Court, the Puisne Indge remarked that 
jhe had frequently commented on the charac- 
jter ofthe lights borne by junksin Hongkong 
“arbour, but without avail. ;What wonder 
was it that ‘accidents occurred under these 
circumstances ? The junks were a menace

SAS OSEBADLER” 


STRANDED ON KENT ROCKS. 
| From Our Corrsspoadent.] 


Singapore, th September, 
: 12.10 p.m. 
The German — cruiser Secaller, 
which left Singapore yesterday even- 
‘ing bound for Colombo, is ashore on 
‘the Kent Rocks, ten miles out, 
” She has not yet got off the rocks. 
[S.M.S. Secadler is a German cruiser of 1,640 
tons and was, Until recently, stationed [1 China 
waters, She had ‘the misfortune of ‘running 
aground on the Trident Shoal at- Labuan, on 
the gist ult. After lightening her'of ber. guns 
and other moveables of heavy weight she:'was 
able to get off the shoal onthe 2nd inst, whence 
she proceeted to Singapore, and on leaving; 
the Southern port last evening a second mishap 
befalls her within a‘fortnight of the first.—Ed.,

another country’; which practically’ destroys 

the value as a legal proposition of the. second 
resumption [think the only way of ascertains 

ing what law is applicable to any given set of 
circumstances is to determine clesrly what the 

: + obligation is which is alleged, to. have ‘bean 
————, broken, if it is connected with the mode of 
performance, as Mr, Dicey suggests, then clear: 

ly the Jaw of the place of performance must 
apply, as in the Credit Lyonnais case where 

the question wax whether the French law of 
force majeure was a sufficient excise for, 
failure to perform a contract in which England 

~ was the place of perlarmance. If the breach 

in this case had arisen in connection with de- 

a livery in Hongkony, then the law of Hongkong 
would apply. . But in this case the breach was 

in connection with the quality of the goods de- 
liveréds the question hetng whether the ‘crack- 

ers were what they. proposed tobe, nr whether, 

to adopt English law terms, the rule as to im- 

plied ‘conditions applied. or the doctrine | of 
‘cavent emptor, The law applicable to this 
obligation must be the law of the place of de- 

» ivery dnd this was, in my opinion, Canton, It 

* was there that the goods were examined and 
accepted by the person who had charge of 

the negotiations for the. plaintiffs, in the pre- 

sence of the delendant :it was there (hat the 
plaintiff decided whether or no he would ac- 

cept the crackers. The transport to Hongkong 

was, it ig true, part of the original contract, but 
thatoccurredafter the question whetherthe goods 

were in accordance with the contract had been 
decided. In the particulats of this preliminary 

- examination the facts vomething tesemble 
those in the Credit Lyonnais case; for there, a5 

; here, there wasto be a preliminary examina- 
5 tion and approval before actual delivery. 
: This was held not to be sufficient to 
disturb the presumption that the law of 

. England = where the contract hid been 
made applied to the contract, In this. 

case by parity of reasoning the fuct that the 
examination was to be made and approval 

given in Canton seems to me to anforce the 

* ‘presumption in favour of the lex loci contractus, 
think therefore that this is a Chinese contract. 

I indicated atthe trial that this might be the 
“view 1 should take; the defendant answered 
that the plaintiff had failed to show what the 

law of Chinais. But itis the defendant who 

has in reality appealed to the Sale of Goods 

*, Ordinance and to the rule cayeat emptor: he 
has falled to show that similar principles are 

. in force in China, [ cannot assume that they 
are, There are, however, some things.that a 

Court may assume: in such a case as the pre- 

cent that itis part of the law of China as of

ed intact, the patching on her bottom not be.

and calm ag the centre of the typhoon passed 
over the-scene was taken advantage of ta make’ 
everything secure and taut again preparatory 
for the outer vortex passing over them and: thal 
had a good denl to do with the absence of all

‘The Council adjourned till the zist inst, 4. | Tite result. was that each “gun” fended’ for 

: himselfand the usually thankless task of looking 
after the food-stuffs and keeping an eye on the 
boys—which is‘ generally relegated to the 
meekeat member of the crowd—was done away 
with. Altogether there is a good deal to be 
said.for this method ; it compels each ane to 
look after himself in the minor concerns of the 
trip and does not impose on the good natured; it 
also tends to reduce expenditure. . 

The cutwatd journey was delightful. On; 
arrival at the venue ‘decided upon, the party 
‘found that no less than four-other launches’ 
wete anchored there, each being filled, with 
sporlsmen. The first ‘night was spent on board, 
and the weather being cool and calmthe sport- 
ing pesple rose with the lark—or in this case 
it might be better.to say they rose with the 
snipe—and started operations. 

Heavy rains had prevailed in the paddy 
fields lalely, with the result that going was very 
heavy. The mud was three feet deep in places, 
and of the consistency of pea‘soup, as: several 
of the “guns” found to their cont when they 
tumbled into a mud hole up to the waist. But 
these litte annoyances, incidental to sport, 
were forzotien in the excellent shooting that 
was obtained, The snipe were flying strong 
in the wing, and the sport was capital, The 
paity had a bag at the end of the day.of 75 
brace of snipe, which was held to besupremely 
satisfactory. The other parties also did well, and 
there is every indication that sport will continue 
good this season, The return journey ‘to 
Hongkong was made th.t same evening, the 
launch: arriving bere at midnight, Ag an ex- 
ample of what the, sportsmen may.expect to 
pay for a couple of days’ shaotiny it may be stat- 
ed thatthe coat of the excursion described, per 
individual, was a few cents over three dollars, 
That paid for the launch, the hire of coolies on 
shore, and general « xpenditure, but did not in- 
clude,the cost of each man’s food, which was 
furnished separately, It will thus be #een that 
the trip may be expected to cos not more than | 
$7 or $8 at the most, unless of course the party 
oes infor luxuries, whith is generally a mis: 
-take on such occasions, 

Lt is some satisfaction ta: know that the re- 
strictions.imposed by the $50 license are no’ at 
present likely to diminish that healthy, love of 
sport which characierises a good many as:is- 
tants in Hongkong. With a $10 license it is 
possible to: get a decent day’s shooting at mo- 
derate expense, which is something to be thank- 
(ul for in the e days when the price of every- 
thing is rising by leaps and bounds.

terday aftgrndon for German East Africa, 

‘The Seeadler -left Singapore at ‘about half- 
past. five o'clock and was ‘soon going nenrly 
full speed, About half-past six, without any 
warning, a violent shock of (he collision was 
felt throughout the ship and the greatest con- 
‘sternation prevailed on board as her bottom 
was heard grating upon a reef. The Seeadier 
hid run on the’Kent Rocks, Engines were 
reversed and every possible means was adopted 
to get her off, but all to no purpose, She was 
hard and fast on the reef. - Her speed carried 
the vessel well oyer the rocks until her 

BOS SHOWED ABOVE WATER, | 


That is to say, so: far as can be ascertained 
up to the present moment, the hull must be on’ 
the rocks to the extent of about half its length, 
‘(he fore part is fixed on the reef and the after 
end is afloat, 2 , 

‘The Kent Rocks are about ten miles from 
Singapore and about halfa mile toa mile dis- 
tant from the ordinary route followed by trad- 
ing and mail steamers. They are ‘submerged, 
being about six feet below water, but they 
have deep water all round—something like 18 
to 20 feet. The Seradler draws about three 
fathoms, ‘She went ashore at high tide. , 

When all attempts to get her off under her 
tw Bleam had failed, she was lightened for- 
ward and preparations made for another effort- 
at refloating her at high water this morning at 
seven o'clock ; and a boat was despatched back 
1o Singapore for assistance. The attempt, 
however, proved unavailing, and cody she 
remains hard and fast on the rocks, : 

When news of the accident. was brought to

new set of social conditions. : Indifference to

“measures for the decrease of humat misery,

and the civil hospital were the principal retuges

I would propose for the consideration ofthe 
Government the adoption of the " Shelterwood 
Strip System,” ‘En this method of felling and 
1e-planting, narrow strips of wood: are felled, 
the remainder being leit standing to protect 
the saplings which are subsequently planted in 
the clearings, No very conspicuous gaps are 
made, the planting areas are easy to find, and 
the adult trees next the saplings can “be felled 
away from them. * : : 

Mall treeswere felled at exactly 35 years 
old jn Hongkong the plantations would be 
J felled as they wore planted, in blocks. If the 
‘| election or shelter-wood systems are adojted 
it'la not possible to cut ‘all trees at the fisht 
age, bit mature blocks should be felled within 
as short a period of years as is possible without 
making conspicuous *gaps,. By cutting and 
re-planting judiciously, homogeneous blocks 
can be siratified so as to yield regular crops of 
adult trees, : rs

A JAPANESE JOURNAL'S CRITICISM 


The NichieNicht devotes an article to the 
subject of the proposed Government Monopoly 
of sugar, of which it disapproves. “The report 
that the Government has under contemplation 
a proposal to make sugar a State Monopoly is 
now being persistently: circulated,” says the 
Tokyo journal, “and, judging’ from what the 
Government has done in schemes of a similar 
nature in the past, it is not at all improbable 
that it may at any moment decide upon such a 
monopoly. In these circumstances, it |s not 
out of place to recard our distinct disapproval 
of the proposed scheme before It is too late. 
The fact that it is fundamentally wrong for a 
Government ‘to engage. in any rofitemaking 
business is widely ‘admitted, the argument 
being that, State intervention or ownership 
militates against tho improvement and progress 
which, would be’ possible if industry ts left to 
(he.-competition of private individuals. The 
inferference of the Government in: business 
undertakings is permissible only where it is 
1 ascensiteted by the exigencies of administra: 
‘}tfon, ‘or the character of the industry is so ex- 
Lténal vepthat it cannot be absolutely necessary 
‘tre @ purposes of taxation, Sugar is ono of 
those commodities the taxation of which should 
be of a simple character, In such countries 
as France, Germany, and Austria-Hungary, 
where the production, of sugar ‘is of great. 
quantity, itis taxed by an ordinary pro- | 
cess, and ‘the same is true even In ussia,

In their circular of the oth inst. Messrs. 
tamke & Rogge remaik :—The past fortnight 
has witnessed the announcement of a: recon- 
ciliation between Japan‘and Russia; but the 
hopes, cherished in many quarters, that imine- 
diately on restoration of, peace the recent pro- 
found depression in‘ freights would be a thing 
Of the past, were doomed ‘to be disappointed. 
The-political event referred to has tended'to-. 
wards an almost general strengthening af ideas 
on the part of owners; unfortunately, however, 
a unifurm aod material improvement on recent 
market conditions has by no means heen | 
created so far, There is at the close, it is (rue, 
a slight. stir inthe North, according -to, 
Shanghai advices, but the Southern market hns 
as yet exhibited litthe change ofany kind. The 
nearathand future has, no doubt, in siore a 
certain outlet for disengaged tonnage in the, 
shape of berth freights hence and from neigh- 
bouring ports ‘to, Viadivostack, as soon as the’ 
latter port is properly prepared for traffic again, 
which does not appear to bs the cise yet. 
“There will in all prebability also be some char... 
teringon monthly basis for Russian account, 
putas faras Japan is concerned, the: latter 
country has since the bepinning of the war s0 
extensively increased her, mercantile fleet 
through purchases and ca:tures in addition to 
building, that she will, though with the task 
before her of transporting back her troops, etc.

‘THe Yellow Déagon for August, which is now 
to hand, is ‘the first number of volume seven of 
this interesting Queen's College publication, 
The head master’s address on “ break-up day” 
i published in full and as it ‘comains many 
instructive suggestions, should be rend and re- 
read'by the stholars, One of the old pupils, 
Hung Hing Kam, who is now studying in Lon- 
don, has written a lengthy letter fo a master, in 
the course of ‘which he expresses the apinion 
that, after what he has seen of English Public 
Schaals, Queen's College, with its ever-increas-

A CASE that came up before Mr. G, N. Orme 
at the Magistracy should be of considerable 
interest to Kowloonites, in view of the many 
complaints recently made of the mal-od rous 
condition of the streets owing. to the promis: 
cuous dumping of household refuse and 
decaying vegetable maiter, which is allowed to, 
lie in the gutters and give out very offensive 
smells. The case brought this morning is, 
we understand, the first of a series ina cam: 
prign, which is being instituted by *he Sanitary 
officials against this casual use of residential 
localities as dumping grounds for all and 
sundry aubbish and refuse. In this case 
Insprcior Lyons caught a hauce-boy, employed 
ig, Carnarvon Road, in the act of dumping a 
quantity ¢f offensive household rubbish at the: 
corner of the road, and immediately proceeded 
against bin, with the result’ that the boy was 
fined $4 or seven days, As this campaign is to 
be vignratsly carried on, Kowloon residents 
should keep an eye on their servants, ather- 
wise the latter may be getting into trouble, 


of an inflammable nature for the Maines to feed 
upon, The place was believed to he insured, 
for the sum of $15,000, but as the master could 
not be fond the actual extent of the insurance, 
as wellasaf the damage, could not be definitely 
ascertained, :

Tue following appears in the Association 
Bulletin, published weekly by the European, 
department of the Young Men’s Christian As- 
sociation, of Hongkong:—There’ are many 
men inthis Colony. who might be enjoying the 
privileges of the Y.M. C. A, but whose atten 
tion, it may be, has never been directed to ard 
them. New men-are continually arriving so 
that it is.almost impossible for a small member- 
ship committee to locate all, or.even a majority 
of them, We'shall urge our present members 
to give their active assistance and to, make ita’ 
point to introduce as.many'new members as: 
possible. We are still below the number, 
Necessary:to the successful working of the 
Association, but trust that the hearty co-opera-, 
tion of the membership will remove this 
difficulty;: Now js a most opportune time to 
join as with the ‘return of cooler weather the 
Association will have much mare tooffer, The 
Glee Club made a good start Jast : Wednesday 
and will hold weekly practices. The Football 
season js about to open, Tecnis courts will 
be provided. Beginning with the first week in 
October there will be a series of social even* 
ings, lectures and debates. Join now,
